Well, I guess... it’s mostly because I keep forgetting things that I really shouldn’t. Important stuff, you know? Like my heart follow-up appointments, or remembering to call the clinic back when they leave a message. It’s frustrating. 

Like, just the other day I went out to fix the hinge on the back gate. You know those heavy wooden ones? They always swell up when it rains, and we had a real downpour last week. Completely flooded the bottom of the driveway. My neighbor Bob actually got a couple of inches of water in his garage from it, ruined a bunch of his old fishing gear he had in cardboard boxes... which is a shame because he used to take me out on his boat back before I retired. 

But anyway, where was I? Oh, right, the gate. I walked all the way out to the fence and realized I didn't even show up with the right tools for the repair job. Planning? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, my garage is a little cluttered, sure, but I don't have trouble organizing things. I know where everything is. Trouble figuring things out? No, no, I don't have any problems like that. I still do the checkbook every month. Well, I say every month, but sometimes I let it slide a week or two. The bank actually just sent me one of those new debit cards in the mail. You know, the ones where you can just tap it on the machine? I went down to the hardware store to buy some WD-40 last week—because the hinges on my back screen door were squeaking like crazy, driving me absolutely nuts every time the wind blew—and I just tapped the card and it paid! Felt like magic. 

The guy working the register was telling me they're gonna start putting chips in people's hands next so you don't even need a wallet. I don't know about all that, sounds a little sci-fi to me. Like that movie with the robots... oh, what was it called? The one from the eighties. I took my wife to see it at the drive-in. Man, I miss drive-in theaters. You used to pay by the carload, pack a whole cooler of sodas and sandwiches in the trunk. We had this big old Ford station wagon back then, the kind with the fake wood paneling on the side. Handled like a boat, but you could fit half the neighborhood in the back. 

But uh... wait, what did you ask me? Fixing things around the house? Yeah, no, I'm fine. Still as handy as ever. I can figure out anything new just fine.

I went down to the hardware store to buy some WD-40 last week—because the hinges on my back screen door were squeaking like crazy, driving me absolutely nuts every time the wind blew—and I just tapped the card and it paid! Felt like magic. \n\nThe guy working the register was telling me they're gonna start putting chips in people's hands next so you don't even need a wallet. I don't know about all that, sounds a little sci-fi to me. Like that movie with the robots... oh, what was it called? The one from the eighties. I took my wife to see it at the drive-in. Man, I miss drive-in theaters. You used to pay by the carload, pack a whole cooler of sodas and sandwiches in the trunk. We had this big old Ford station wagon back then, the kind with the fake wood paneling on the side. Handled like a boat, but you could fit half the neighborhood in the back. \n\nBut uh... wait, what did you ask me? Fixing things around the house? Yeah, no, I'm fine. Still as handy as ever. I can figure out anything new just fine.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had trouble keeping track of multiple things at once or managing tasks that involve more than one step?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 68 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Keeping track of multiple things? No, no, I'm sharp as a tack with that stuff. I mean, just yesterday I was making my famous three-bean chili. You gotta do a lot of things at once for that—browning the meat, chopping the onions, getting the spices just right. I always use a little dash of cinnamon, that's the secret. Learned that from a guy I used to work with down at the plant. Frank. Frank was a real character, let me tell you. He used to bring in these massive Tupperware containers of food every single shift. 

He had this little Jack Russell terrier, too, that would ride in the passenger seat of his truck every morning. Cutest little thing, but man, it had a lot of energy. Just bounced off the walls of that cab. Kind of like my grandson, actually. They came to visit last Christmas and that boy just could not sit still during dinner. Kept kicking the table leg. My wife—God rest her soul—she would have had a fit, but I just let him be. Kids are just like that now, you know? I blame the food. There's too much sugar in everything. Even the ketchup! Have you ever actually looked at the back of a ketchup bottle? It's all high fructose corn syrup. I started buying that organic stuff for a while, but it just doesn't taste the same on a hot dog. You go to a baseball game, you want regular ketchup and mustard. I haven't been to a game in years, though. The tickets are just too expensive now. Used to be you could get a bleacher seat for five bucks, get a hot dog and a beer, and still have change from a twenty... 

Wait, what was the question? Something about steps? Oh, right, managing tasks. No, I don't have any trouble with that. I can juggle a dozen things at once just fine.

We planted that tree... gosh, it must have been thirty years ago. My wife and I brought it home in the back of my old Chevy pickup. That was a great truck. Two-tone blue and white. They don't make them like that anymore, all steel, you know? Now everything's plastic and computer chips. If something breaks on a car these days, you can't even fix it yourself, you gotta take it to a dealership and they plug it into a machine. It's ridiculous. I used to change my own oil, do the brake pads, everything. Taught my son how to do it, too, right there in the driveway. He lives up in Seattle now, works for one of those tech companies. He's always trying to get me to use a smartphone, but I just like my regular flip phone. It makes calls, what else do I need? \n\nBut, uh... time of day? No, I got a watch right here. It's afternoon. I don't get confused about that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble figuring out how to do something you've done many times before, like balancing the checkbook, fixing something around the house, or learning how to use a new appliance?\n\nPatient: Trouble figuring things out? No, no, I don't have any problems like that. I still do the checkbook every month. Well, I say every month, but sometimes I let it slide a week or two. The bank actually just sent me one of those new debit cards in the mail. You know, the ones where you can just tap it on the machine? I went down to the hardware store to buy some WD-40 last week—because the hinges on my back screen door were squeaking like crazy, driving me absolutely nuts every time the wind blew—and I just tapped the card and it paid! Felt like magic. \n\nThe guy working the register was telling me they're gonna start putting chips in people's hands next so you don't even need a wallet. I don't know about all that, sounds a little sci-fi to me. Like that movie with the robots... oh, what was it called? The one from the eighties. I took my wife to see it at the drive-in. Man, I miss drive-in theaters. You used to pay by the carload, pack a whole cooler of sodas and sandwiches in the trunk. We had this big old Ford station wagon back then, the kind with the fake wood paneling on the side. Handled like a boat, but you could fit half the neighborhood in the back. \n\nBut uh... wait, what did you ask me? Fixing things around the house? Yeah, no, I'm fine. Still as handy as ever. I can figure out anything new just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble keeping track of multiple things at once or managing tasks that involve more than one step?\n\nPatient: Keeping track of multiple things? No, no, I'm sharp as a tack with that stuff. I mean, just yesterday I was making my famous three-bean chili. You gotta do a lot of things at once for that—browning the meat, chopping the onions, getting the spices just right. I always use a little dash of cinnamon, that's the secret. Learned that from a guy I used to work with down at the plant. Frank. Frank was a real character, let me tell you. He used to bring in these massive Tupperware containers of food every single shift. \n\nHe had this little Jack Russell terrier, too, that would ride in the passenger seat of his truck every morning. Cutest little thing, but man, it had a lot of energy. Just bounced off the walls of that cab. Kind of like my grandson, actually. They came to visit last Christmas and that boy just could not sit still during dinner. Kept kicking the table leg. My wife—God rest her soul—she would have had a fit, but I just let him be. Kids are just like that now, you know? I blame the food. There's too much sugar in everything. Even the ketchup! Have you ever actually looked at the back of a ketchup bottle? It's all high fructose corn syrup. I started buying that organic stuff for a while, but it just doesn't taste the same on a hot dog. You go to a baseball game, you want regular ketchup and mustard. I haven't been to a game in years, though. The tickets are just too expensive now. Used to be you could get a bleacher seat for five bucks, get a hot dog and a beer, and still have change from a twenty... \n\nWait, what was the question? Something about steps? Oh, right, managing tasks. No, I don't have any trouble with that. I can juggle a dozen things at once just fine.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your ability to make sound decisions or judgments, such as managing money wisely or knowing what to do in an emergency?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

Changes in making decisions? No, no, I'm good there. I've always been careful with my money, especially now that I'm retired. You really have to stretch a dollar these days. I mean, I went to the grocery store yesterday to pick up some eggs, and I almost fell over when I saw the price. It's highway robbery! And the coffee, too. I always buy those big red plastic tubs of Folgers. My wife, she used to wash those tubs out and use them to store nuts and bolts out in the garage. I still have a whole shelf of them out there. 

I was actually just out in the garage digging through them on Sunday because the pull cord on my lawnmower snapped right off in my hand. Just broke clean off! So I had to drive down to the hardware store to get a replacement rope, and guess who I ran into? Jimmy from the old deli down on Main Street. They tore his place down years ago to build that big ugly pharmacy. Shame, too, because he made the best pastrami sandwich in town. Piled high, you know? Anyway, he said he's moving down to Florida next month. Everybody's moving to Florida. I couldn't do it, too much humidity. My hair gets all frizzy and I sweat like a pig. 

Wait, what did you ask me? Something about an emergency? Yeah, no, I'm fine in a pinch. Still got a good head on my shoulders. I haven't noticed any problems with my judgment.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Memory problems? Well, I don't know if I'd call it a big issue, but... well, actually, maybe it is getting a little out of hand. It's not just normal forgetfulness, I guess. I mean, it’s gotten to where the guys at the senior center repair group can’t count on me the way they used to. I used to be the one they’d call first when someone brought in a broken toaster or a lamp that needed rewiring. I loved fixing those old lamps. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore, you know? They used real brass back in the day, heavy stuff. Now everything is just cheap plastic from overseas. You buy a lamp at the big box store, it lasts a year, and then you just throw it away. It's terrible for the environment. 

My daughter is always on my case about recycling. She bought me these three different colored bins for the kitchen—one for paper, one for plastic, one for glass. Takes up half the floor space! I told her, 'Honey, I grew up in a time when milk came in glass bottles and the milkman picked up the empties.' We didn't need special bins, we just reused things. The milkman we had, Mr. Higgins, he drove this little white truck with no doors on it. He'd just hop in and out. And he always had a pocket full of peppermint candies for the neighborhood kids. I don't think kids even like peppermints anymore. It's all those sour gummy worms now. I tried one of those once, nearly turned my mouth inside out...

But, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, the memory thing. Yeah, so the repair group is frustrated with me, and... oh, and my doctor’s office. They’ve had to chase me down lately about missed follow-ups for my heart. I just completely forget the appointments. So I guess it is causing some real problems.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... it’s mostly because I keep forgetting things that I really shouldn’t. Important stuff, you know? Like my heart follow-up appointments, or remembering to call the clinic back when they leave a message. It’s frustrating. \n\nLike, just the other day I went out to fix the hinge on the back gate. You know those heavy wooden ones? They always swell up when it rains, and we had a real downpour last week. Completely flooded the bottom of the driveway. My neighbor Bob actually got a couple of inches of water in his garage from it, ruined a bunch of his old fishing gear he had in cardboard boxes... which is a shame because he used to take me out on his boat back before I retired. \n\nBut anyway, where was I? Oh, right, the gate. I walked all the way out to the fence and realized I didn't even show up with the right tools for the repair job. Just standing there with a flathead screwdriver when I needed the drill. So, yeah, that kind of thing keeps happening, and it's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes besides your memory, like trouble with planning, organizing, or following directions?\n\nPatient: Planning? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, my garage is a little cluttered, sure, but I don't have trouble organizing things. I know where everything is. Actually, I was just out there trying to organize my socket wrenches the other day because I bought this old red metal toolbox at a yard sale last weekend. Only paid five bucks for it! \n\nThe lady selling it said it belonged to her husband who used to work down at the old auto plant. You know, the one off Route 9? That place closed down back in... what, '98? '99? It really did a number on the local economy when it shut its doors. A lot of good guys lost their jobs. My brother-in-law was one of them, actually. He ended up having to pack up and move all the way out to Arizona just to find work. It is way too hot out there for me, I'll tell you that. I went to visit him once in July and I thought I was gonna melt right there on his patio. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what were you asking me? Oh, right, following directions. No, no trouble with that. I can read a map or follow instructions just fine, so long as I remember what I'm doing in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel confused about where you are, what day it is, or have trouble recognizing familiar places or people?\n\nPatient: Confused about where I am? No, no, I know exactly where I am. And I know it's... well, it's Tuesday, right? Yeah, Tuesday. I always know it's Tuesday because that's the day the garbage trucks come around. You know, they used to come on Thursdays, but they changed the whole route system last year. Something about optimizing fuel consumption, I think. The new driver they got, though, he's a real piece of work. Drives that big truck like he's in the Indy 500. Just last week, he clipped the edge of Mrs. Higgins' mailbox. Didn't knock it over, but left a big old scrape on the side. She was furious. She's been meaning to paint that mailbox anyway, she told me she bought this nice hunter green paint from the hardware store downtown. You know the one? Next to the bakery? Best cherry danishes in town, I swear. I try not to eat too many sweets these days, doctor's orders and all that... wait, what did you ask me? Recognizing people? Oh, no, I know everyone. Never had a problem with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself confused about the current date, season, or time of day?\n\nPatient: No, no, I always know what day it is. Like I said, it's Tuesday. And it's definitely spring, you can tell by the pollen. My car was covered in this thick yellow dust this morning. It's that big oak tree in the front yard. We planted that tree... gosh, it must have been thirty years ago. My wife and I brought it home in the back of my old Chevy pickup. That was a great truck. Two-tone blue and white. They don't make them like that anymore, all steel, you know? Now everything's plastic and computer chips. If something breaks on a car these days, you can't even fix it yourself, you gotta take it to a dealership and they plug it into a machine. It's ridiculous. I used to change my own oil, do the brake pads, everything. Taught my son how to do it, too, right there in the driveway. He lives up in Seattle now, works for one of those tech companies. He's always trying to get me to use a smartphone, but I just like my regular flip phone. It makes calls, what else do I need? \n\nBut, uh... time of day? No, I got a watch right here. It's afternoon. I don't get confused about that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble figuring out how to do something you've done many times before, like balancing the checkbook, fixing something around the house, or learning how to use a new appliance?\n\nPatient: Trouble figuring things out? No, no, I don't have any problems like that. I still do the checkbook every month. Well, I say every month, but sometimes I let it slide a week or two. The bank actually just sent me one of those new debit cards in the mail. You know, the ones where you can just tap it on the machine? I went down to the hardware store to buy some WD-40 last week—because the hinges on my back screen door were squeaking like crazy, driving me absolutely nuts every time the wind blew—and I just tapped the card and it paid! Felt like magic. \n\nThe guy working the register was telling me they're gonna start putting chips in people's hands next so you don't even need a wallet. I don't know about all that, sounds a little sci-fi to me. Like that movie with the robots... oh, what was it called? The one from the eighties. I took my wife to see it at the drive-in. Man, I miss drive-in theaters. You used to pay by the carload, pack a whole cooler of sodas and sandwiches in the trunk. We had this big old Ford station wagon back then, the kind with the fake wood paneling on the side. Handled like a boat, but you could fit half the neighborhood in the back. \n\nBut uh... wait, what did you ask me? Fixing things around the house? Yeah, no, I'm fine. Still as handy as ever. I can figure out anything new just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble keeping track of multiple things at once or managing tasks that involve more than one step?\n\nPatient: Keeping track of multiple things? No, no, I'm sharp as a tack with that stuff. I mean, just yesterday I was making my famous three-bean chili. You gotta do a lot of things at once for that—browning the meat, chopping the onions, getting the spices just right. I always use a little dash of cinnamon, that's the secret. Learned that from a guy I used to work with down at the plant. Frank. Frank was a real character, let me tell you. He used to bring in these massive Tupperware containers of food every single shift. \n\nHe had this little Jack Russell terrier, too, that would ride in the passenger seat of his truck every morning. Cutest little thing, but man, it had a lot of energy. Just bounced off the walls of that cab. Kind of like my grandson, actually. They came to visit last Christmas and that boy just could not sit still during dinner. Kept kicking the table leg. My wife—God rest her soul—she would have had a fit, but I just let him be. Kids are just like that now, you know? I blame the food. There's too much sugar in everything. Even the ketchup! Have you ever actually looked at the back of a ketchup bottle? It's all high fructose corn syrup. I started buying that organic stuff for a while, but it just doesn't taste the same on a hot dog. You go to a baseball game, you want regular ketchup and mustard. I haven't been to a game in years, though. The tickets are just too expensive now. Used to be you could get a bleacher seat for five bucks, get a hot dog and a beer, and still have change from a twenty... \n\nWait, what was the question? Something about steps? Oh, right, managing tasks. No, I don't have any trouble with that. I can juggle a dozen things at once just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to make sound decisions or judgments, such as managing money wisely or knowing what to do in an emergency?\n\nPatient: Changes in making decisions? No, no, I'm good there. I've always been careful with my money, especially now that I'm retired. You really have to stretch a dollar these days. I mean, I went to the grocery store yesterday to pick up some eggs, and I almost fell over when I saw the price. It's highway robbery! And the coffee, too. I always buy those big red plastic tubs of Folgers. My wife, she used to wash those tubs out and use them to store nuts and bolts out in the garage. I still have a whole shelf of them out there. \n\nI was actually just out in the garage digging through them on Sunday because the pull cord on my lawnmower snapped right off in my hand. Just broke clean off! So I had to drive down to the hardware store to get a replacement rope, and guess who I ran into? Jimmy from the old deli down on Main Street. They tore his place down years ago to build that big ugly pharmacy. Shame, too, because he made the best pastrami sandwich in town. Piled high, you know? Anyway, he said he's moving down to Florida next month. Everybody's moving to Florida. I couldn't do it, too much humidity. My hair gets all frizzy and I sweat like a pig. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Something about an emergency? Yeah, no, I'm fine in a pinch. Still got a good head on my shoulders. I haven't noticed any problems with my judgment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that the memory problems you're experiencing are a big issue, or do you think it's just normal forgetfulness that happens with age?\n\nPatient: Memory problems? Well, I don't know if I'd call it a big issue, but... well, actually, maybe it is getting a little out of hand. It's not just normal forgetfulness, I guess. I mean, it’s gotten to where the guys at the senior center repair group can’t count on me the way they used to. I used to be the one they’d call first when someone brought in a broken toaster or a lamp that needed rewiring. I loved fixing those old lamps. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore, you know? They used real brass back in the day, heavy stuff. Now everything is just cheap plastic from overseas. You buy a lamp at the big box store, it lasts a year, and then you just throw it away. It's terrible for the environment. \n\nMy daughter is always on my case about recycling. She bought me these three different colored bins for the kitchen—one for paper, one for plastic, one for glass. Takes up half the floor space! I told her, 'Honey, I grew up in a time when milk came in glass bottles and the milkman picked up the empties.' We didn't need special bins, we just reused things. The milkman we had, Mr. Higgins, he drove this little white truck with no doors on it. He'd just hop in and out. And he always had a pocket full of peppermint candies for the neighborhood kids. I don't think kids even like peppermints anymore. It's all those sour gummy worms now. I tried one of those once, nearly turned my mouth inside out...\n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, the memory thing. Yeah, so the repair group is frustrated with me, and... oh, and my doctor’s office. They’ve had to chase me down lately about missed follow-ups for my heart. I just completely forget the appointments. So I guess it is causing some real problems.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I told her, 'Honey, I grew up in a time when milk came in glass bottles and the milkman picked up the empties.' We didn't need special bins, we just reused things. The milkman we had, Mr. Higgins, he drove this little white truck with no doors on it. He'd just hop in and out. And he always had a pocket full of peppermint candies for the neighborhood kids. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Breaking down a task into steps? Planning a meal? No, no, I've got no trouble with that. I mean, I'm a planner. Always have been. You should see how I organize my garage. Every tool has its place. Speaking of tools, I was looking for my good set of socket wrenches the other day. The ones with the red handles. I got those at a Sears going-out-of-business sale. Man, I miss Sears. You could get anything there—clothes, appliances, tires. My dad bought his first riding mower from Sears back in, gosh, must have been '72 or '73. It was this bright green Craftsman. Built like a tank. He used to let me steer it while he worked the pedals. 

We had this huge yard back then, practically an acre. Took all afternoon to mow. And the smell of fresh-cut grass, there’s nothing like it. Although, my neighbor now, he hires this landscaping company that comes at 7:00 AM on a Saturday with those loud gas blowers. Wakes up the whole neighborhood. I don't know why people can't just use a rake anymore. It's good exercise! I try to walk at least a mile a day myself. Gotta keep the joints moving, you know? My knees aren't what they used to be. The left one clicks when I go up the stairs. The doctor said it's just wear and tear, but I think it’s from playing catcher in high school. You squat like that for hours, it does a number on your cartilage...

Wait, what were we talking about? Planning? Yeah, no, I don't have any trouble organizing things or figuring out steps. I'm perfectly fine with all that.

Never had a problem with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself confused about the current date, season, or time of day?\n\nPatient: No, no, I always know what day it is. Like I said, it's Tuesday. And it's definitely spring, you can tell by the pollen. My car was covered in this thick yellow dust this morning. It's that big oak tree in the front yard. We planted that tree... gosh, it must have been thirty years ago. My wife and I brought it home in the back of my old Chevy pickup. That was a great truck. Two-tone blue and white. They don't make them like that anymore, all steel, you know? Now everything's plastic and computer chips. If something breaks on a car these days, you can't even fix it yourself, you gotta take it to a dealership and they plug it into a machine. It's ridiculous. I used to change my own oil, do the brake pads, everything. Taught my son how to do it, too, right there in the driveway. He lives up in Seattle now, works for one of those tech companies. He's always trying to get me to use a smartphone, but I just like my regular flip phone. It makes calls, what else do I need? \n\nBut, uh... time of day? No, I got a watch right here. It's afternoon. I don't get confused about that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble figuring out how to do something you've done many times before, like balancing the checkbook, fixing something around the house, or learning how to use a new appliance?\n\nPatient: Trouble figuring things out? No, no, I don't have any problems like that. I still do the checkbook every month. Well, I say every month, but sometimes I let it slide a week or two. The bank actually just sent me one of those new debit cards in the mail. You know, the ones where you can just tap it on the machine? I went down to the hardware store to buy some WD-40 last week—because the hinges on my back screen door were squeaking like crazy, driving me absolutely nuts every time the wind blew—and I just tapped the card and it paid! Felt like magic. \n\nThe guy working the register was telling me they're gonna start putting chips in people's hands next so you don't even need a wallet. I don't know about all that, sounds a little sci-fi to me. Like that movie with the robots... oh, what was it called? The one from the eighties. I took my wife to see it at the drive-in. Man, I miss drive-in theaters. You used to pay by the carload, pack a whole cooler of sodas and sandwiches in the trunk. We had this big old Ford station wagon back then, the kind with the fake wood paneling on the side. Handled like a boat, but you could fit half the neighborhood in the back. \n\nBut uh... wait, what did you ask me? Fixing things around the house? Yeah, no, I'm fine. Still as handy as ever. I can figure out anything new just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble keeping track of multiple things at once or managing tasks that involve more than one step?\n\nPatient: Keeping track of multiple things? No, no, I'm sharp as a tack with that stuff. I mean, just yesterday I was making my famous three-bean chili. You gotta do a lot of things at once for that—browning the meat, chopping the onions, getting the spices just right. I always use a little dash of cinnamon, that's the secret. Learned that from a guy I used to work with down at the plant. Frank. Frank was a real character, let me tell you. He used to bring in these massive Tupperware containers of food every single shift. \n\nHe had this little Jack Russell terrier, too, that would ride in the passenger seat of his truck every morning. Cutest little thing, but man, it had a lot of energy. Just bounced off the walls of that cab. Kind of like my grandson, actually. They came to visit last Christmas and that boy just could not sit still during dinner. Kept kicking the table leg. My wife—God rest her soul—she would have had a fit, but I just let him be. Kids are just like that now, you know? I blame the food. There's too much sugar in everything. Even the ketchup! Have you ever actually looked at the back of a ketchup bottle? It's all high fructose corn syrup. I started buying that organic stuff for a while, but it just doesn't taste the same on a hot dog. You go to a baseball game, you want regular ketchup and mustard. I haven't been to a game in years, though. The tickets are just too expensive now. Used to be you could get a bleacher seat for five bucks, get a hot dog and a beer, and still have change from a twenty... \n\nWait, what was the question? Something about steps? Oh, right, managing tasks. No, I don't have any trouble with that. I can juggle a dozen things at once just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to make sound decisions or judgments, such as managing money wisely or knowing what to do in an emergency?\n\nPatient: Changes in making decisions? No, no, I'm good there. I've always been careful with my money, especially now that I'm retired. You really have to stretch a dollar these days. I mean, I went to the grocery store yesterday to pick up some eggs, and I almost fell over when I saw the price. It's highway robbery! And the coffee, too. I always buy those big red plastic tubs of Folgers. My wife, she used to wash those tubs out and use them to store nuts and bolts out in the garage. I still have a whole shelf of them out there. \n\nI was actually just out in the garage digging through them on Sunday because the pull cord on my lawnmower snapped right off in my hand. Just broke clean off! So I had to drive down to the hardware store to get a replacement rope, and guess who I ran into? Jimmy from the old deli down on Main Street. They tore his place down years ago to build that big ugly pharmacy. Shame, too, because he made the best pastrami sandwich in town. Piled high, you know? Anyway, he said he's moving down to Florida next month. Everybody's moving to Florida. I couldn't do it, too much humidity. My hair gets all frizzy and I sweat like a pig. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Something about an emergency? Yeah, no, I'm fine in a pinch. Still got a good head on my shoulders. I haven't noticed any problems with my judgment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that the memory problems you're experiencing are a big issue, or do you think it's just normal forgetfulness that happens with age?\n\nPatient: Memory problems? Well, I don't know if I'd call it a big issue, but... well, actually, maybe it is getting a little out of hand. It's not just normal forgetfulness, I guess. I mean, it’s gotten to where the guys at the senior center repair group can’t count on me the way they used to. I used to be the one they’d call first when someone brought in a broken toaster or a lamp that needed rewiring. I loved fixing those old lamps. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore, you know? They used real brass back in the day, heavy stuff. Now everything is just cheap plastic from overseas. You buy a lamp at the big box store, it lasts a year, and then you just throw it away. It's terrible for the environment. \n\nMy daughter is always on my case about recycling. She bought me these three different colored bins for the kitchen—one for paper, one for plastic, one for glass. Takes up half the floor space! I told her, 'Honey, I grew up in a time when milk came in glass bottles and the milkman picked up the empties.' We didn't need special bins, we just reused things. The milkman we had, Mr. Higgins, he drove this little white truck with no doors on it. He'd just hop in and out. And he always had a pocket full of peppermint candies for the neighborhood kids. I don't think kids even like peppermints anymore. It's all those sour gummy worms now. I tried one of those once, nearly turned my mouth inside out...\n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, the memory thing. Yeah, so the repair group is frustrated with me, and... oh, and my doctor’s office. They’ve had to chase me down lately about missed follow-ups for my heart. I just completely forget the appointments. So I guess it is causing some real problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble breaking down a task into steps, like planning a meal or organizing a project?\n\nPatient: Breaking down a task into steps? Planning a meal? No, no, I've got no trouble with that. I mean, I'm a planner. Always have been. You should see how I organize my garage. Every tool has its place. Speaking of tools, I was looking for my good set of socket wrenches the other day. The ones with the red handles. I got those at a Sears going-out-of-business sale. Man, I miss Sears. You could get anything there—clothes, appliances, tires. My dad bought his first riding mower from Sears back in, gosh, must have been '72 or '73. It was this bright green Craftsman. Built like a tank. He used to let me steer it while he worked the pedals. \n\nWe had this huge yard back then, practically an acre. Took all afternoon to mow. And the smell of fresh-cut grass, there’s nothing like it. Although, my neighbor now, he hires this landscaping company that comes at 7:00 AM on a Saturday with those loud gas blowers. Wakes up the whole neighborhood. I don't know why people can't just use a rake anymore. It's good exercise! I try to walk at least a mile a day myself. Gotta keep the joints moving, you know? My knees aren't what they used to be. The left one clicks when I go up the stairs. The doctor said it's just wear and tear, but I think it’s from playing catcher in high school. You squat like that for hours, it does a number on your cartilage...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Planning? Yeah, no, I don't have any trouble organizing things or figuring out steps. I'm perfectly fine with all that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Trouble judging distances or getting lost? No, not at all. I know my way around town like the back of my hand. I've lived here my whole life, you know. Actually, speaking of driving, I was just taking my old Buick down to the car wash on Tuesday. It’s one of those classic ones where they hand-dry it at the end. You don't see those much anymore. Usually, it's just those big automated tunnels with the spinning brushes that scratch up your paint. My buddy Phil took his new truck through one of those and came out with a huge scrape down the passenger side. He was furious. 

Phil’s always getting into something, though. Last week, he tried to fix a leak in his roof himself instead of calling a guy. Slipped on a wet shingle and nearly broke his collarbone. I told him, 'Phil, we’re not spring chickens anymore, you gotta leave that stuff to the professionals.' But he’s stubborn. Reminds me of my old man. My dad would refuse to ask for directions, even if we were driving around in circles for hours on family vacations. We went to Mount Rushmore once back in '64, and I swear we saw the same cornfield in South Dakota about six times before he finally pulled into a gas station. Gas was, what, thirty cents a gallon back then? And they pumped it for you, washed your windshield, checked your oil. The attendants wore those neat little uniforms with the matching hats...

What did you ask me? Something about left and right? Yeah, no, I don't have any issues with that. My sense of direction is sharp as a tack. I never get lost.

He's always trying to get me to use a smartphone, but I just like my regular flip phone. It makes calls, what else do I need? \n\nBut, uh... time of day? No, I got a watch right here. It's afternoon. I don't get confused about that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble figuring out how to do something you've done many times before, like balancing the checkbook, fixing something around the house, or learning how to use a new appliance?\n\nPatient: Trouble figuring things out? No, no, I don't have any problems like that. I still do the checkbook every month. Well, I say every month, but sometimes I let it slide a week or two. The bank actually just sent me one of those new debit cards in the mail. You know, the ones where you can just tap it on the machine? I went down to the hardware store to buy some WD-40 last week—because the hinges on my back screen door were squeaking like crazy, driving me absolutely nuts every time the wind blew—and I just tapped the card and it paid! Felt like magic. \n\nThe guy working the register was telling me they're gonna start putting chips in people's hands next so you don't even need a wallet. I don't know about all that, sounds a little sci-fi to me. Like that movie with the robots... oh, what was it called? The one from the eighties. I took my wife to see it at the drive-in. Man, I miss drive-in theaters. You used to pay by the carload, pack a whole cooler of sodas and sandwiches in the trunk. We had this big old Ford station wagon back then, the kind with the fake wood paneling on the side. Handled like a boat, but you could fit half the neighborhood in the back. \n\nBut uh... wait, what did you ask me? Fixing things around the house? Yeah, no, I'm fine. Still as handy as ever. I can figure out anything new just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble keeping track of multiple things at once or managing tasks that involve more than one step?\n\nPatient: Keeping track of multiple things? No, no, I'm sharp as a tack with that stuff. I mean, just yesterday I was making my famous three-bean chili. You gotta do a lot of things at once for that—browning the meat, chopping the onions, getting the spices just right. I always use a little dash of cinnamon, that's the secret. Learned that from a guy I used to work with down at the plant. Frank. Frank was a real character, let me tell you. He used to bring in these massive Tupperware containers of food every single shift. \n\nHe had this little Jack Russell terrier, too, that would ride in the passenger seat of his truck every morning. Cutest little thing, but man, it had a lot of energy. Just bounced off the walls of that cab. Kind of like my grandson, actually. They came to visit last Christmas and that boy just could not sit still during dinner. Kept kicking the table leg. My wife—God rest her soul—she would have had a fit, but I just let him be. Kids are just like that now, you know? I blame the food. There's too much sugar in everything. Even the ketchup! Have you ever actually looked at the back of a ketchup bottle? It's all high fructose corn syrup. I started buying that organic stuff for a while, but it just doesn't taste the same on a hot dog. You go to a baseball game, you want regular ketchup and mustard. I haven't been to a game in years, though. The tickets are just too expensive now. Used to be you could get a bleacher seat for five bucks, get a hot dog and a beer, and still have change from a twenty... \n\nWait, what was the question? Something about steps? Oh, right, managing tasks. No, I don't have any trouble with that. I can juggle a dozen things at once just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to make sound decisions or judgments, such as managing money wisely or knowing what to do in an emergency?\n\nPatient: Changes in making decisions? No, no, I'm good there. I've always been careful with my money, especially now that I'm retired. You really have to stretch a dollar these days. I mean, I went to the grocery store yesterday to pick up some eggs, and I almost fell over when I saw the price. It's highway robbery! And the coffee, too. I always buy those big red plastic tubs of Folgers. My wife, she used to wash those tubs out and use them to store nuts and bolts out in the garage. I still have a whole shelf of them out there. \n\nI was actually just out in the garage digging through them on Sunday because the pull cord on my lawnmower snapped right off in my hand. Just broke clean off! So I had to drive down to the hardware store to get a replacement rope, and guess who I ran into? Jimmy from the old deli down on Main Street. They tore his place down years ago to build that big ugly pharmacy. Shame, too, because he made the best pastrami sandwich in town. Piled high, you know? Anyway, he said he's moving down to Florida next month. Everybody's moving to Florida. I couldn't do it, too much humidity. My hair gets all frizzy and I sweat like a pig. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Something about an emergency? Yeah, no, I'm fine in a pinch. Still got a good head on my shoulders. I haven't noticed any problems with my judgment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that the memory problems you're experiencing are a big issue, or do you think it's just normal forgetfulness that happens with age?\n\nPatient: Memory problems? Well, I don't know if I'd call it a big issue, but... well, actually, maybe it is getting a little out of hand. It's not just normal forgetfulness, I guess. I mean, it’s gotten to where the guys at the senior center repair group can’t count on me the way they used to. I used to be the one they’d call first when someone brought in a broken toaster or a lamp that needed rewiring. I loved fixing those old lamps. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore, you know? They used real brass back in the day, heavy stuff. Now everything is just cheap plastic from overseas. You buy a lamp at the big box store, it lasts a year, and then you just throw it away. It's terrible for the environment. \n\nMy daughter is always on my case about recycling. She bought me these three different colored bins for the kitchen—one for paper, one for plastic, one for glass. Takes up half the floor space! I told her, 'Honey, I grew up in a time when milk came in glass bottles and the milkman picked up the empties.' We didn't need special bins, we just reused things. The milkman we had, Mr. Higgins, he drove this little white truck with no doors on it. He'd just hop in and out. And he always had a pocket full of peppermint candies for the neighborhood kids. I don't think kids even like peppermints anymore. It's all those sour gummy worms now. I tried one of those once, nearly turned my mouth inside out...\n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, the memory thing. Yeah, so the repair group is frustrated with me, and... oh, and my doctor’s office. They’ve had to chase me down lately about missed follow-ups for my heart. I just completely forget the appointments. So I guess it is causing some real problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble breaking down a task into steps, like planning a meal or organizing a project?\n\nPatient: Breaking down a task into steps? Planning a meal? No, no, I've got no trouble with that. I mean, I'm a planner. Always have been. You should see how I organize my garage. Every tool has its place. Speaking of tools, I was looking for my good set of socket wrenches the other day. The ones with the red handles. I got those at a Sears going-out-of-business sale. Man, I miss Sears. You could get anything there—clothes, appliances, tires. My dad bought his first riding mower from Sears back in, gosh, must have been '72 or '73. It was this bright green Craftsman. Built like a tank. He used to let me steer it while he worked the pedals. \n\nWe had this huge yard back then, practically an acre. Took all afternoon to mow. And the smell of fresh-cut grass, there’s nothing like it. Although, my neighbor now, he hires this landscaping company that comes at 7:00 AM on a Saturday with those loud gas blowers. Wakes up the whole neighborhood. I don't know why people can't just use a rake anymore. It's good exercise! I try to walk at least a mile a day myself. Gotta keep the joints moving, you know? My knees aren't what they used to be. The left one clicks when I go up the stairs. The doctor said it's just wear and tear, but I think it’s from playing catcher in high school. You squat like that for hours, it does a number on your cartilage...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Planning? Yeah, no, I don't have any trouble organizing things or figuring out steps. I'm perfectly fine with all that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble with things like judging distances, telling left from right, or finding your way around places you know well?\n\nPatient: Trouble judging distances or getting lost? No, not at all. I know my way around town like the back of my hand. I've lived here my whole life, you know. Actually, speaking of driving, I was just taking my old Buick down to the car wash on Tuesday. It’s one of those classic ones where they hand-dry it at the end. You don't see those much anymore. Usually, it's just those big automated tunnels with the spinning brushes that scratch up your paint. My buddy Phil took his new truck through one of those and came out with a huge scrape down the passenger side. He was furious. \n\nPhil’s always getting into something, though. Last week, he tried to fix a leak in his roof himself instead of calling a guy. Slipped on a wet shingle and nearly broke his collarbone. I told him, 'Phil, we’re not spring chickens anymore, you gotta leave that stuff to the professionals.' But he’s stubborn. Reminds me of my old man. My dad would refuse to ask for directions, even if we were driving around in circles for hours on family vacations. We went to Mount Rushmore once back in '64, and I swear we saw the same cornfield in South Dakota about six times before he finally pulled into a gas station. Gas was, what, thirty cents a gallon back then? And they pumped it for you, washed your windshield, checked your oil. The attendants wore those neat little uniforms with the matching hats...\n\nWhat did you ask me? Something about left and right? Yeah, no, I don't have any issues with that. My sense of direction is sharp as a tack. I never get lost.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Recognizing faces or objects? No, I haven't noticed anything like that. I mean, my eyesight isn't what it was when I was twenty, but I can still spot a cardinal at the bird feeder from the kitchen window. I put out sunflower seeds for them. The squirrels try to get into it, though. Those little rascals are smart. I bought one of those "squirrel-proof" feeders—cost me forty bucks at the hardware store. Supposed to spin around and throw them off when they grab it. Well, it took the squirrels about three days to figure out how to hang from the branch above it and scoop the seeds out with one paw. You gotta admire their persistence. 

It's like my grandson, Tommy. When he wants something, he just won't quit. He’s been begging his mother for a dog for two years now. A golden retriever, specifically. He even made a whole presentation on her iPad with pictures and a list of chores he promised to do. Kids today and their technology... I didn't even know what an iPad was until my daughter got me one for Christmas. Mostly I just use it to play Solitaire. The cards are big enough I don't need my reading glasses. 

But, uh, where was I going with that? Oh, recognizing things. Yeah, no, I see fine. Faces, objects, maps... well, nobody uses paper maps anymore anyway, it's all on the phone now. But no, I don't have any trouble with any of that.

They don't make them like that anymore, all steel, you know? Now everything's plastic and computer chips. I don't know about all that, sounds a little sci-fi to me. Like that movie with the robots... oh, what was it called? The one from the eighties. I took my wife to see it at the drive-in. Man, I miss drive-in theaters. You used to pay by the carload, pack a whole cooler of sodas and sandwiches in the trunk. We had this big old Ford station wagon back then, the kind with the fake wood paneling on the side. Handled like a boat, but you could fit half the neighborhood in the back. \n\nBut uh... wait, what did you ask me? Fixing things around the house? Yeah, no, I'm fine. Still as handy as ever. No, no, I'm sharp as a tack with that stuff. I mean, just yesterday I was making my famous three-bean chili. You gotta do a lot of things at once for that—browning the meat, chopping the onions, getting the spices just right. I always use a little dash of cinnamon, that's the secret. Learned that from a guy I used to work with down at the plant. Frank. Frank was a real character, let me tell you. He used to bring in these massive Tupperware containers of food every single shift. \n\nHe had this little Jack Russell terrier, too, that would ride in the passenger seat of his truck every morning. Cutest little thing, but man, it had a lot of energy. Just bounced off the walls of that cab. Kind of like my grandson, actually. They came to visit last Christmas and that boy just could not sit still during dinner. Kept kicking the table leg. My wife—God rest her soul—she would have had a fit, but I just let him be. Kids are just like that now, you know? I blame the food. There's too much sugar in everything. Even the ketchup! Have you ever actually looked at the back of a ketchup bottle? It's all high fructose corn syrup. I started buying that organic stuff for a while, but it just doesn't taste the same on a hot dog. You go to a baseball game, you want regular ketchup and mustard. I haven't been to a game in years, though. The tickets are just too expensive now. Used to be you could get a bleacher seat for five bucks, get a hot dog and a beer, and still have change from a twenty... \n\nWait, what was the question? Something about steps? Oh, right, managing tasks. No, I don't have any trouble with that. I can juggle a dozen things at once just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to make sound decisions or judgments, such as managing money wisely or knowing what to do in an emergency?\n\nPatient: Changes in making decisions? No, no, I'm good there. I've always been careful with my money, especially now that I'm retired. You really have to stretch a dollar these days. I mean, I went to the grocery store yesterday to pick up some eggs, and I almost fell over when I saw the price. It's highway robbery! And the coffee, too. I always buy those big red plastic tubs of Folgers. My wife, she used to wash those tubs out and use them to store nuts and bolts out in the garage. I still have a whole shelf of them out there. \n\nI was actually just out in the garage digging through them on Sunday because the pull cord on my lawnmower snapped right off in my hand. Just broke clean off! So I had to drive down to the hardware store to get a replacement rope, and guess who I ran into? Jimmy from the old deli down on Main Street. They tore his place down years ago to build that big ugly pharmacy. Shame, too, because he made the best pastrami sandwich in town. Piled high, you know? Anyway, he said he's moving down to Florida next month. Everybody's moving to Florida. I couldn't do it, too much humidity. My hair gets all frizzy and I sweat like a pig. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Something about an emergency? Yeah, no, I'm fine in a pinch. Still got a good head on my shoulders. I haven't noticed any problems with my judgment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that the memory problems you're experiencing are a big issue, or do you think it's just normal forgetfulness that happens with age?\n\nPatient: Memory problems? Well, I don't know if I'd call it a big issue, but... well, actually, maybe it is getting a little out of hand. It's not just normal forgetfulness, I guess. I mean, it’s gotten to where the guys at the senior center repair group can’t count on me the way they used to. I used to be the one they’d call first when someone brought in a broken toaster or a lamp that needed rewiring. I loved fixing those old lamps. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore, you know? They used real brass back in the day, heavy stuff. Now everything is just cheap plastic from overseas. You buy a lamp at the big box store, it lasts a year, and then you just throw it away. It's terrible for the environment. \n\nMy daughter is always on my case about recycling. She bought me these three different colored bins for the kitchen—one for paper, one for plastic, one for glass. Takes up half the floor space! I told her, 'Honey, I grew up in a time when milk came in glass bottles and the milkman picked up the empties.' We didn't need special bins, we just reused things. The milkman we had, Mr. Higgins, he drove this little white truck with no doors on it. He'd just hop in and out. And he always had a pocket full of peppermint candies for the neighborhood kids. I don't think kids even like peppermints anymore. It's all those sour gummy worms now. I tried one of those once, nearly turned my mouth inside out...\n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, the memory thing. Yeah, so the repair group is frustrated with me, and... oh, and my doctor’s office. They’ve had to chase me down lately about missed follow-ups for my heart. I just completely forget the appointments. So I guess it is causing some real problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble breaking down a task into steps, like planning a meal or organizing a project?\n\nPatient: Breaking down a task into steps? Planning a meal? No, no, I've got no trouble with that. I mean, I'm a planner. Always have been. You should see how I organize my garage. Every tool has its place. Speaking of tools, I was looking for my good set of socket wrenches the other day. The ones with the red handles. I got those at a Sears going-out-of-business sale. Man, I miss Sears. You could get anything there—clothes, appliances, tires. My dad bought his first riding mower from Sears back in, gosh, must have been '72 or '73. It was this bright green Craftsman. Built like a tank. He used to let me steer it while he worked the pedals. \n\nWe had this huge yard back then, practically an acre. Took all afternoon to mow. And the smell of fresh-cut grass, there’s nothing like it. Although, my neighbor now, he hires this landscaping company that comes at 7:00 AM on a Saturday with those loud gas blowers. Wakes up the whole neighborhood. I don't know why people can't just use a rake anymore. It's good exercise! I try to walk at least a mile a day myself. Gotta keep the joints moving, you know? My knees aren't what they used to be. The left one clicks when I go up the stairs. The doctor said it's just wear and tear, but I think it’s from playing catcher in high school. You squat like that for hours, it does a number on your cartilage...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Planning? Yeah, no, I don't have any trouble organizing things or figuring out steps. I'm perfectly fine with all that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble with things like judging distances, telling left from right, or finding your way around places you know well?\n\nPatient: Trouble judging distances or getting lost? No, not at all. I know my way around town like the back of my hand. I've lived here my whole life, you know. Actually, speaking of driving, I was just taking my old Buick down to the car wash on Tuesday. It’s one of those classic ones where they hand-dry it at the end. You don't see those much anymore. Usually, it's just those big automated tunnels with the spinning brushes that scratch up your paint. My buddy Phil took his new truck through one of those and came out with a huge scrape down the passenger side. He was furious. \n\nPhil’s always getting into something, though. Last week, he tried to fix a leak in his roof himself instead of calling a guy. Slipped on a wet shingle and nearly broke his collarbone. I told him, 'Phil, we’re not spring chickens anymore, you gotta leave that stuff to the professionals.' But he’s stubborn. Reminds me of my old man. My dad would refuse to ask for directions, even if we were driving around in circles for hours on family vacations. We went to Mount Rushmore once back in '64, and I swear we saw the same cornfield in South Dakota about six times before he finally pulled into a gas station. Gas was, what, thirty cents a gallon back then? And they pumped it for you, washed your windshield, checked your oil. The attendants wore those neat little uniforms with the matching hats...\n\nWhat did you ask me? Something about left and right? Yeah, no, I don't have any issues with that. My sense of direction is sharp as a tack. I never get lost.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty recognizing objects or faces, or understanding visual information, like reading a map or judging distances while driving?\n\nPatient: Recognizing faces or objects? No, I haven't noticed anything like that. I mean, my eyesight isn't what it was when I was twenty, but I can still spot a cardinal at the bird feeder from the kitchen window. I put out sunflower seeds for them. The squirrels try to get into it, though. Those little rascals are smart. I bought one of those \"squirrel-proof\" feeders—cost me forty bucks at the hardware store. Supposed to spin around and throw them off when they grab it. Well, it took the squirrels about three days to figure out how to hang from the branch above it and scoop the seeds out with one paw. You gotta admire their persistence. \n\nIt's like my grandson, Tommy. When he wants something, he just won't quit. He’s been begging his mother for a dog for two years now. A golden retriever, specifically. He even made a whole presentation on her iPad with pictures and a list of chores he promised to do. Kids today and their technology... I didn't even know what an iPad was until my daughter got me one for Christmas. Mostly I just use it to play Solitaire. The cards are big enough I don't need my reading glasses. \n\nBut, uh, where was I going with that? Oh, recognizing things. Yeah, no, I see fine. Faces, objects, maps... well, nobody uses paper maps anymore anyway, it's all on the phone now. But no, I don't have any trouble with any of that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Following complicated conversations? No, no, I follow along just fine. I mean, I watch the evening news every night at 6:30. Tom Brokaw used to be my guy, but now it's Lester Holt. He does a good job. Always looks so sharp in those suits. I used to wear a suit to work every single day when I was at the insurance company. Had a closet full of them—navy, charcoal, a nice tan one for the summer. You had to wear a tie, too. None of this "casual Friday" business they have now. My nephew, he works in tech out in California, and he goes to the office in a t-shirt and sneakers! Can you imagine? 

He makes good money, though. Just bought a house with a pool. I told him, 'A pool is a hole in the ground you pour money into.' I had an above-ground one when my kids were little. The filter was always breaking down. I spent half my weekends skimming leaves out of that thing. The oak tree in the backyard dropped leaves all year round, I swear. Problems with my social life? Well, I'm retired, so I don't have a job to go to anymore. But I do help out with the repair group down at the senior center. Or, well, I try to. It’s gotten to where they can’t really count on me the way they used to because I just forget to show up or bring the right tools. 

Speaking of repairs, I was trying to fix my toaster the other day. It's one of those heavy old chrome ones, built like an absolute tank. You buy a toaster today at the big box store, it's all cheap plastic and the heating element burns out in six months. My mother had a toaster that lasted thirty years. She used to make us toast with this homemade strawberry jam she’d can every summer. The whole house would smell like strawberries and sugar. We had a huge garden out back... tomatoes, green beans, and always way too much zucchini. You’d be trying to give it away to all the neighbors, leaving it on their porches! Old Mrs. Gable next door made a fantastic zucchini bread with hers. She passed away a few years back, bless her heart, and the young couple that bought her house just ripped out all her beautiful rose bushes. Such a shame. Roses take a lot of work, though. You gotta prune them just right or they get all leggy... 

Anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, memory causing problems. Yeah, the repair group gets a bit frustrated with me. And my regular doctor’s office, too—they've had to chase me down a bunch of times lately about missed heart follow-ups. I just completely lose track of the days and miss the appointments. They send those little reminder cards in the mail, but they just get lost in the pile of catalogs and junk mail on the kitchen counter. Who even buys things from catalogs anymore?

It's one of those heavy old chrome ones, built like an absolute tank. You buy a toaster today at the big box store, it's all cheap plastic and the heating element burns out in six months. Worse? Oh, yeah, definitely worse. It's been causing real trouble for about a year and two months now. That's right around when I had my cardiac scare. They had me in the hospital for three days, hooked up to all those monitors. The food was terrible, let me tell you. Dry chicken and these steamed carrots that had no flavor at all. I snuck down to the cafeteria one afternoon just to get a decent cup of coffee. Hospital coffee is like drinking brown water. 

Anyway, after that scare, the doctor told me I had to take it easy. Forced me to cut back on my daily walks and all the projects I used to do around the house. I used to walk three miles every morning, rain or shine. Had this great pair of walking shoes, New Balance. White with the blue 'N' on the side. They don't make them like they used to, though. I bought a new pair last year and the sole started peeling off after a month! I tried gluing it back with some epoxy I had in the garage, but it never held right. 

But yeah, since I had to stop the walking and the projects... those things used to keep me settled, you know? Burned off that energy. Now I'm just sitting around more, and the memory stuff has just gotten so much worse since then. Like I can't keep a thought in my head for two minutes.

Always looks so sharp in those suits. I used to wear a suit to work every single day when I was at the insurance company. Had a closet full of them—navy, charcoal, a nice tan one for the summer. You had to wear a tie, too. None of this \"casual Friday\" business they have now. My nephew, he works in tech out in California, and he goes to the office in a t-shirt and sneakers! Well, I'm retired, so I don't have a job to go to anymore. But I do help out with the repair group down at the senior center. Or, well, I try to. It’s gotten to where they can’t really count on me the way they used to because I just forget to show up or bring the right tools. \n\nSpeaking of repairs, I was trying to fix my toaster the other day. Trouble figuring out steps? No, I don't think so. Like with a new gadget, I usually just dive right in. My daughter actually bought me one of those new smart TVs for my birthday last month. Huge thing, 55 inches. It barely fit in the back of her SUV. Found it right away, too, sitting right next to my old tackle box. 

I haven't been fishing in years, not since my buddy Earl moved down to Florida. He lives in one of those retirement communities right on a golf course now. I can read a map or follow instructions just fine, so long as I remember what I'm doing in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel confused about where you are, what day it is, or have trouble recognizing familiar places or people?\n\nPatient: Confused about where I am? No, no, I know exactly where I am. And I know it's... well, it's Tuesday, right? Yeah, Tuesday. I always know it's Tuesday because that's the day the garbage trucks come around. The new driver they got, though, he's a real piece of work. Drives that big truck like he's in the Indy 500. Just last week, he clipped the edge of Mrs. Higgins' mailbox. Didn't knock it over, but left a big old scrape on the side. She was furious. She's been meaning to paint that mailbox anyway, she told me she bought this nice hunter green paint from the hardware store downtown. You know the one? Next to the bakery? Best cherry danishes in town, I swear. I try not to eat too many sweets these days, doctor's orders and all that... wait, what did you ask me? Recognizing people? Oh, no, I know everyone. Never had a problem with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself confused about the current date, season, or time of day?\n\nPatient: No, no, I always know what day it is. Like I said, it's Tuesday. And it's definitely spring, you can tell by the pollen. My car was covered in this thick yellow dust this morning. It's that big oak tree in the front yard. We planted that tree... gosh, it must have been thirty years ago. My wife and I brought it home in the back of my old Chevy pickup. That was a great truck. Two-tone blue and white. They don't make them like that anymore, all steel, you know? Now everything's plastic and computer chips. If something breaks on a car these days, you can't even fix it yourself, you gotta take it to a dealership and they plug it into a machine. It's ridiculous.
